Since Hu sought a favour, and Gu was involved, there was no reason to refuse. He gladly accepted and brought a substantial present: an exceptionally large root of old wild ginseng from Jilin. Zeng treated him as an honoured guest. After prolonged compliments and civilities, he summoned the accountant, a cultivated middle-aged man. Conversation revealed that Wu was a licentiate. Accounting in an opium firm seemed a waste of his talents.

“Opium is extracted from the poppy,” Wu began. “Poppies have existed in China since ancient times, particularly in Sichuan. Su Dongpo, who came from Sichuan, wrote: ‘The Daoist urges a draught of mint; the boy can boil a poppy broth.’ Honey was added, and it was taken as a tonic for the lungs and stomach.” “A scholar indeed!” Hu remarked. “His first words are poetry.” “Mr Wu,” Gu said, “we need not begin so far back. Let us confine ourselves to imported opium.” Imports first arrived during the Chenghua reign of the Ming. By Wanli’s reign, a duty had been prescribed. It was used medicinally, being considered unfailingly effective against dysentery. Smoking it, however, was prohibited in the late Ming and early Qing. Strict maritime restrictions also kept imports low. In the twenty-third year of Kangxi, those restrictions were eased, and opium could again enter as a medicine. The duty was slight: two mace of silver on every ten jin. As smoking gradually spread, Yongzheng issued a prohibition. But as the saying went, “The more illicit salt is forbidden, the better it sells.” Opium was the same: stricter prohibitions brought more smuggling. From the thirty-eighth year of Qianlong, when Britain established the East India Company and made opium exports a source of state revenue, smuggling became still worse.

The result was “white exchanged for black”: opium came in and silver went out. Before the thirtieth year of Qianlong, annual opium imports had amounted to only two or three hundred chests. By the end of the reign they reached a thousand. At the beginning of Daoguang they stood at four thousand, increasing within ten years to over twenty-three thousand. Before Daoguang’s third year, the silver smuggled out amounted to only a few million taels; by his eighteenth year it had risen to thirty million. That figure concerned Guangdong alone. Tens of millions more left through ports in Fujian, Zhejiang, Shandong, and Tianjin. The country’s economic lifeblood was at stake, and the Opium War followed. “Formal permission to import and tax it came in the seventh year of Xianfeng,” Wu continued. “Wang Yide, then governor-general of Fujian and Zhejiang, pleaded urgent military needs and temporary expediency. Faced with the Taiping rebellion, the court could only agree. A treaty with France followed the next year, fixing the import duty at thirty taels per hundred jin. Since it entered as medicine, imported opium was called ‘foreign medicine’; that grown in Yunnan and Sichuan was ‘native medicine.’ Both paid likin when transported and sold inland, quite separately from the import duty.” Zuo, however, considered customs duty and likin effectively the same. He advocated restraining consumption through taxation, charging a combined hundred and fifty taels per hundred jin. Hu asked Wu whether separate collection or a consolidated charge was preferable.

“Consolidation is better,” Wu replied. “Likin is assessed on value. Native opium is cheap, foreign opium expensive. If the foreign product is passed off as native, the revenue falls.” “Quite right. A simple and clear principle, yet someone unfamiliar with the trade would never think of it.” Hu was pleased. “Thank you. To borrow a literary phrase, I have ‘benefited greatly.’” Whenever he came to Shanghai, Hu made a point of visiting Weiyuan Teahouse on Baoshan Street, carrying plenty of banknotes in denominations of ten and twenty taels. It was an expression of his kindness and fidelity to old friends. Those doing well he met over wine in the high-class courtesan houses. Those he saw at Weiyuan were generally less fortunate. He asked after their circumstances and, according to their needs, quietly slipped a couple of notes into their hands. He also inquired after absent friends and sent assistance through others. Several old acquaintances therefore regularly called at Fukang or the Transport Bureau to ask whether Mr Hu had arrived.

There were many old friends at Weiyuan that day, most having learned of his visit beforehand. As Hu greeted them one after another, ten o’clock arrived unnoticed. Gu reminded him, “It is almost time, Little Uncle. You must not be late for this appointment.” Naturally Hu would not keep Li waiting. He had calculated carefully: the appointment was at eleven, and the temple was only a quarter of an hour from Weiyuan. There was ample time. “Still early!” “No, let us go to the Transport Bureau first. I met a friend here who gave me some important news. We ought to discuss it.” “Very well. I intended to change there anyway.” They went together and conferred privately in Hu’s office. “A friend from the customs told me that nearly all the provincial remittances have arrived. Any shortfall is small. But apparently Shao Xiaocun means to hold the silver back for a while. Short-term silver rates have risen sharply, and he wants to earn some interest.” Hu nodded and considered. “The interest cannot amount to much. Shao is hardly so greedy for a small profit. There may be another scheme behind it.” “Whatever it is, we ought to approach him soon.” “No. If he means to play tricks, the timing makes no difference. I shall simply not approach him.” “Then what do you intend to do?” Gu asked in surprise. “I have not decided. We shall discuss it after I return from the temple.”

Hu changed into official dress and presented his card at the temple. Li asked him to change into informal clothes before meeting. Still in mourning, Li wore a pale-blue bamboo-cloth robe and a black cloth riding jacket. He looked worn and haggard. Hu naturally offered condolences. Remembering Hu’s funeral gift of a thousand taels, Li thanked him specially. It had been too generous, he said, but as returning it would have been awkward, he had donated it to a charitable institution. After lengthy civilities, they turned to business. “Opium has caused harm for a long time. But its import duty is a major source of revenue for the ministry’s treasury. Instead of claiming that taxation will restrain consumption, we should speak frankly: what matters is increasing revenue. That is the practical approach.” His opening directly contradicted Zuo’s principle. Hu could say nothing beyond an acknowledgment.

“Higher rates are not the best way to increase revenue. Plugging the leaks is the fundamental remedy,” Li continued. “When Shanghai introduced new opium regulations in the eleventh year of Tongzhi, the Prussian consul protested that additional likin obstructed foreign trade in China. That argument was unreasonable. Likin was our affair; what did it have to do with foreign merchants paying import duty? The Zongli Yamen rejected his objection. But Hart pointed out that heavier likin meant more smuggling. With contraband entering through obscure channels, legitimate foreign merchants also lost business. In that sense, the claim that higher likin harmed foreign trade was true.” “I hear the contraband comes from Hong Kong,” Hu said. “Exactly. Here is a statement you may examine.” Li took a sheet from the low table on the kang and handed it over. Hu rose hastily, received it with both hands, and sat down to read. From the thirteenth year of Tongzhi to the fourth of Guangxu, between eighty-four thousand and ninety-six thousand chests of foreign opium had reached Hong Kong annually; only sixty-five thousand to seventy-one thousand subsequently entering the various ports had paid duty. In Guangxu’s fifth year, a hundred and seven thousand arrived at Hong Kong, but only eighty-six thousand paid duty. Every year, smuggled imports exceeded twenty thousand chests. “Import duty is thirty taels per chest, and the fixed likin twenty, apart from unofficial local charges. Altogether it comes to roughly eighty taels a chest. Twenty thousand smuggled chests mean a revenue loss of one million six hundred thousand taels.” Li changed direction abruptly. “Hart has now agreed to consolidate and raise the charges: eighty taels in addition to the regular thirty. He will also help China prevent smuggling. These negotiations have produced a satisfactory result.” “No one in China handles foreign affairs better than you, Your Excellency,” Hu replied flatteringly. “Hart has always respected you.”

“Foreigners are manageable enough. They are formidable, but they listen to reason. What troubles me most is personal rivalry among our own people. That is why I have asked you here.” He plainly meant Zuo. Hu gave a vague acknowledgment without expressing an opinion. “Please explain to Marquis Zuo: he advocates a combined charge of a hundred and fifty taels a chest. Hart says he can agree if China insists, but cannot then guarantee the prevention of smuggling. Xueyan, suppose annual imports are a hundred thousand chests, of which twenty-five thousand are smuggled. Calculate the difference.” Hu calculated quickly. A hundred thousand chests at a hundred and ten taels yielded eleven million taels. Seventy-five thousand taxed at a hundred and fifty yielded eleven million two hundred and fifty thousand—still two hundred and fifty thousand more. “Two hundred and fifty thousand is a small matter. Preventing smuggling is far more important. Only with Hart’s guarantee can our sovereignty be complete. Otherwise, when smuggling grows and we remonstrate, he will say he warned us and can do nothing. What answer shall we have? Please persuade Marquis Zuo not to dispute the matter further. The present situation is poor, with powerful enemies pressing upon us. We need smooth negotiations so that our energy and time can go where they are needed. What do you think?” “Your consideration for the country could not be more thorough. I shall certainly impress this upon His Excellency Zuo. He will appreciate your pains.” “Then I must rely upon your assistance.”

“You are too kind.” Hu seized the opportunity to introduce his own concern. “Speaking of foreign dealings, particularly now that we need British support against France, there is something on which I must ask your help.” “What is it?” “The HSBC loan will soon fall due. I hear nearly all the provincial military subsidies have arrived; any shortfall must be small. Would you instruct Xiaocun to release the funds promptly and make up any slight deficiency? Ready silver is scarce in Shanghai. Only the customs can ease matters by releasing some of its reserves. If Xiaocun helps, His Excellency Zuo will appreciate it too.” “I shall ask him.” Li put it admirably: “It is all public business, all for the country. There should be no distinction between your side and mine.” His actions matched his words. That afternoon Hu received a letter from Shao: only four hundred and seventy thousand taels of provincial remittances had arrived, but he had been instructed to advance the balance. Hu should send someone to arrange withdrawal.

“Repayment is not due until the end of the month,” Mi Benchang said happily. “We can use these funds for a few days. With silver rates so high, there is a small profit to be made.” “Do not chase a petty gain.” Hu had another plan. “Arrange a transfer tomorrow. Ask them to draw a draft on HSBC, transfer that draft directly, and recover the sealed voucher. Let us make a handsome job of it.” Mi was a man who could, as the saying went, turn somersaults through a cash coin’s hole. He thought Hu foolish to sacrifice the interest, but his employer’s instructions had to be obeyed. Early the next morning he completed the transfer and brought back the voucher bearing the official seal of the governor-general of Shaanxi and Gansu. Hu cancelled it and instructed Secretary Zhu, the Transport Bureau’s drafting clerk, to write to Zuo. Besides reporting the repayment, the letter was to set out Li’s request fully and emphatically, stressing in conclusion that Li had behaved generously and urging Zuo to return the courtesy.

The letter was important, and Hu supervised its composition himself. It was finished after four that afternoon. Just as he was about to visit the Seventh Lady, Gu arrived with an invitation in his hand. “HSBC’s comprador, Zeng Yousheng, delivered this personally and asked me to bring it to you. He invites you to dinner tonight and particularly hopes you will come.” Hu seemed to have the matter in hand. His first question was, “Who else is invited?” “Only me, to keep you company.” “Where?” “At Taili in Hongkou.” “Is that not a restaurant frequented only by foreigners?” “Yes. I think he chose this French restaurant, where Chinese people do not usually go, so that you could speak freely.” Hu reflected, stroked his moustache, and smiled. “It seems I shall not have to raise the subject myself.” “What had you intended to discuss with him?” “What do you think?” Gu considered carefully. “I understand.”

CHAPTER SIX

HSBC’s comprador, Zeng Yousheng, was a snob who liked to bolster his own importance with foreign power. His attentiveness to Hu arose chiefly from the fact that Hu could walk straight into the bank manager’s office and deal with him directly, whether or not they had met before. Zeng dared not withhold respect; Hu, however, disliked precisely this snobbery. This time Zeng had come on his manager’s instructions. The bank had just received five hundred thousand taels in ready silver and needed to put it to work. Would Hu care to borrow it? “Money is very tight in the market. You could lend it to someone else. Why ask a banker like me?” “Money is tight, certainly, but the situation is unfavourable. We must choose our borrowers carefully. For credit, your name stands above all others.” Zeng smiled ingratiatingly. “Such an opportunity seldom comes, Mr Hu. Please give me a helping hand.” “You flatter me, Brother Yousheng. HSBC’s comprador is the one who helps others along. Who is qualified to help you?” “You are. There is no pretending with a man like you. HSBC dares lend this money to no one else. Only you can help me.” “Since you put it that way, I shall do whatever a friend can, so far as it lies within my power. But I do not understand what help you want.”

“Only permission to add a little to the interest for myself,” Zeng said frankly. “Could you use the whole five hundred thousand?” “You fear risks, and so do I.” Hu deliberately turned to Gu. “Minister Wang insists on depositing two hundred thousand taels with us. Can we turn him down?” Gu had no idea who this “Minister Wang” was, but understood the intention: Hu wanted to suggest that Fukang had ample funds. He therefore feigned puzzlement. “Did you not see yesterday’s telegram from Beijing?” “No. What did it say?” “Minister Wang’s two hundred thousand has already been deposited: half in Beijing and half in Tianjin. Has Mi not told you?” “He has been terribly busy today. He must have forgotten.” Hu looked at Zeng. “The silk-buying season is almost over too. This is rather difficult.” “With your resources, a few hundred thousand more would scarcely matter. Shanghai is full of rumours, but business inland is sound. Besides, the lean fifth and sixth months, between the old harvest and the new, are approaching. If Fukang has funds, it need never fear being unable to lend them. Think it over. Taking this money can only help you.” Hu nodded, paused, and asked the rate.

“A round one,” Zeng said. “But I shall report only point eight-five to the bank. That is fair enough, surely?” “Annual or monthly?” “Monthly, of course.” “One per cent a month means twelve per cent a year. There is nothing fair about that.” “You cannot compare present conditions with the old days. Besides, other lenders deduct something at the outset. You will receive this sum in full.” Hu did not readily believe him, but, considered fairly, the loan could hardly be called unattractive. They bargained back and forth and finally agreed upon ten per cent a year, payable half-yearly, for a two-year term with an option to extend for another year. Hu did not ask what additional margin Zeng would arrange in his account with HSBC; he would simply acknowledge the figures Zeng presented.

Hu had already intended to borrow this money, just as HSBC had intended to lend it to him. Their wishes coincided. His handsome gesture of repaying early instead of pursuing a small interest gain had strengthened the manager’s conviction that he was a first-class customer. Zeng therefore arranged the loan without difficulty. Six months’ interest was deducted in advance. Once the proceeds had been transferred to Fukang, Hu personally drew a draft for Zeng’s benefit and had Gu deliver it. Even Mi knew nothing of this private side of the transaction. News travelled quickly among bankers. At the senior bankers’ gathering in Yuyuan at the City God Temple the next morning, Mi’s table was unusually crowded with men hoping to borrow silver. But he kept a tight hold on it. Hu had instructed that, apart from covering Gu’s outstanding bad debts, the money was reserved for another purpose. “Business depends on recognizing opportunities,” Hu said. “A poor market can be an opportunity too. It requires judgment: judge correctly and you make a fortune; judge wrongly and you lose your capital. With money tight, everyone is selling goods for cash. How do you think we should use these funds?” Gu favoured stocking up on tea; Mi suggested land. Hu rejected both for one reason: either investment would depend on subsequent developments and could not produce an immediate effect.

“In an unfavourable situation, nothing will produce an effect,” Mi argued. “Short-term lending seems best.” “We shall not discuss that. We did not open a bank merely to profit from other bankers. As for producing an effect, circumstances matter, but strength matters more. If you already have enough, a small addition will make the difference.” Hu took three teacups and filled one. “These two are only half full. They are like tea and land: still a long way from the brim. Add more to this full cup, however, and it overflows at once. That is what happens when your existing strength is sufficient.” Gu understood. “Four ounces moving a thousand jin. But you have more than one full cup, Little Uncle. Which do you intend to make overflow?” “What do you think?” “Silk?” “Exactly.”

Gu strongly disagreed. Hu already held enormous stocks of silk. The foreign market was poor that year, and overseas buyers offered low prices. Hu refused to sell, tying up much ready silver; there was no reason to invest still more. “No, Yingchun. Their prices are low because they have miscalculated. They think I want to sell for cash and mean to buy cheaply. There is still silk available elsewhere, so they are in no hurry. What about me? Do you think I am in a hurry?” The sudden question left Gu and Mi at a loss. “Well? Why do you say nothing?” “I do not know about you,” Mi said, “but I am certainly anxious.” “So am I. How could I not be? But showing anxiety only makes matters worse. Once people know you are desperate, they wait to take advantage of you. With the HSBC loan, I said Minister Wang had deposited a large sum and we had plenty of money. That made Zeng all the more eager to lend, and gave us an advantage over the rate. Had I let slip how much I wanted it, you would have seen quite another face.” “I do not think the principle applies in every case,” Gu said. He felt Hu’s calculations were wrong but could not find where to begin his objection.

“You say we cannot generalize. I say the principle is the same. While prices are low, I shall buy up all the silk on the market. Unable to find supplies, the foreigners will naturally return to me.” “Suppose both sides hold out: one refuses to sell because the price is too low, the other refuses to buy because it is too high. You must remember, Little Uncle, that you will suffer, not they.” “Why should I?” “Does silk not turn yellow?” “Certainly. But it only turns yellow. Bleaching will take some trouble; it will not become so useless that we must throw it into the sea. If we hold out against each other, I am on my own ground and can find some way through. The foreigners who come for supplies cannot return empty-handed. Without raw materials, their mills must close. I do not believe they can outlast me. If they really unite to force my price down, I still have one last inspired move to turn defeat into survival.” Both men wanted to hear it. Hu merely smiled and said no more, as though the remark had been an offhand evasion intended to conceal a weakness.

Alone with Gu, however, his manner changed. “I have considered your objections, Yingchun.” He seemed agitated. “Abroad, especially in Britain, merchants rank among the foremost people. Here we have scholars, farmers, artisans, and merchants, and merchants are called the lowest of the four classes. Now it is better still: ‘Every merchant is a rogue.’ If I had not understood how official life works, I should never have reached my present position. Is that not the truth?” “Not necessarily. In business alone, you would still be a first-rate man.” “First-rate among merchants, you mean—not among the four classes. Do not lose your head, Yingchun, and imagine your business ability is so remarkable. However great it is, it cannot surpass the foreigners’, especially the British. Why? Because a whole country is doing business against you. Take a foreign loan. Once the terms are agreed, the British minister intervenes and requires an official undertaking from the Zongli Yamen. Failing to repay a British merchant becomes the same as failing to repay the queen. Try it! Their gentler method is to hold the customs in their hands. Their harsher method is to bring warships off your port and aim their guns at the busiest parts of your city. It is a debt to the King of Hell. Dare you refuse? They will take your life!” Hu normally spoke calmly; Gu had never heard such sharpness. He dared neither agree nor contradict, since either response would only drive Hu further.

Hu paid no attention to the reason for his silence. The words had reached his lips and would not be held back. “When Mr Wu described the British government’s opium trade, I had much to reflect upon. Outwardly it is the East India Company that grows the opium; in reality, the government directs everything. The slightest injury to the company’s interests brings government intervention. Its profits go to the government, so that is understandable. But silk? British merchants trade in it entirely on their own account. Their profits and losses have nothing to do with the government. Yet it still intervenes, claiming that our cocoon levies are too high and increase British buyers’ costs, and demanding reductions. Their government speaks for its merchants at every turn. What about ours, Yingchun?” “Must I say it?” Gu replied with a bitter smile. “As the saying goes, you need not know good goods from bad if you can compare them. Governments are the same. Some say our Qing dynasty is much better than the Ming. With the absurd conduct of the emperors and eunuchs at the end of the Ming, there would have been no justice in Heaven had the dynasty survived. But you must compare three shops, not two. Granted, the Qing is better than the Ming; we must compare it with other countries as well. That is the third shop. Which can it match? Not Britain, France, America, or Germany—not even Japan, in my view.” “You are straying from the subject, Little Uncle.” “Then back to business. I know where the court has helped me. Official deposits at low interest give my bank an advantage. But I earned that by helping the court. It favours Hu the red-buttoned official who has rendered service, not Hu the great merchant. There is a distinction. Do you agree?” “Your argument is rather profound today.” Gu rubbed his temples. “Let me think.” “Do. We cannot proceed until you understand.”

Gu carefully considered the distinction, then asked, “Do you mean the court should support large-scale business?” “Yes—business that competes with foreigners. It should support everyone selling to the foreign market. That is warfare with foreigners, though without swords and guns.” “Indeed. There is a new term for it: commercial warfare. That is what you mean.” “Exactly. While I fight this commercial war, the court stands watching, sometimes jeering or pulling me back. Can I defeat the other side?” “Of course not.” “There!” Hu suddenly raised his voice. “That is where I differ from others. Knowing I cannot win, I mean to fight all the same. And”—his words were deliberate—“I mean to vindicate myself before the court and make those great gentlemen feel ashamed.” “Then it is obstinacy, not self-respect,” Gu said with a smile. “They are much the same. Well-directed obstinacy becomes self-respect; without understanding how to vindicate yourself, it is merely obstinacy.” “Well said. But enough talk: how do you mean to prove yourself? If you fail and drop the stone on your own foot, even obstinacy will be too flattering a name.” This brought them back to Hu’s inspired last resort. He had consistently avoided discussing the plan; in truth, he had never thought it through seriously. Now he had to think and speak.

“At worst, I can buy up several modern silk-reeling mills and produce the silk myself.” Gu could scarcely believe his ears. Hu had always opposed modern mills. Such a complete reversal seemed extraordinary. After a little reflection, however, Gu thought the idea excellent. He had always differed from Hu on this question. Knowing foreign languages and dealing frequently with foreigners, he understood Western developments better. Traditional reeling was costly and produced inferior silk; it should long ago have been superseded. Hu’s concern for rural silk households had made him resist modern methods. Now he had not merely abandoned his opposition but proposed entering the industry himself. Gu was both astonished and delighted. “Even if the foreigners were not opposing you, Little Uncle, you ought to do this. Consider—” Eagerly he set out the reasons for changing course. First, modern machinery worked several times faster than hand- and foot-operated equipment. Cocoons could be bought in any quantity and sent straight to the mills. No warehouses would be needed for dried cocoons, nor would there be fear of living pupae emerging through them. Second, the finished silk’s evenness, cleanliness, and lustre far surpassed the traditional product. Third, buying the cocoons, reeling the silk, and selling it abroad oneself would place the entire operation under one direction. Foreign competition need no longer be feared; indeed, foreigners would have no means of competing. These arguments, especially the last, moved Hu considerably. Still unable to decide, he answered only, “We shall see. There is no great urgency.”

Gu thought otherwise. Action should begin at once. Hu held large stocks of dried cocoons. If he had them reeled by traditional methods and could not agree on a price with foreign buyers, the silk would sit in warehouses and turn yellow. With mills of his own and direct foreign sales, Gu believed, there would be little risk. He therefore actively investigated the modern mills. There were five. The earliest, Baochang, had been founded by a Frenchman named Brunat. Next came the Qichang mill, attached to the American firm of Russell & Company. The third, Gongheyong, had opened only the previous year under Huang Zuoqing of Huzhou. Jardine’s and Gongping Trading House also operated mills, as Russell’s did. All five were of roughly similar size and all were unprofitable, for two reasons. Supplies of dried cocoons were unreliable, frequently leaving machinery idle. Nor was the machinery’s capacity fully realized, and the finished silk fell short of expectations. It was said that Gongheyong, Jardine’s, and Gongping intended jointly to engage a renowned Italian technician to supervise operations. The other two seemed ready to abandon the business. If Hu wished to buy, this was the opportunity.

Gu was enthusiastic. He first consulted the Seventh Lady about how best to put the matter to Hu. “I do not know much about modern reeling mills,” she said, “but even an outsider can see that machine-reeled silk is better than the traditional kind.”

“Good products will find buyers,” Gu said. “Little Uncle insists on the best in every business. Now the best is plainly available, yet he refuses it. That is strange.” The Seventh Lady considered. “I shall speak to him.” “It is not that I do not want it, Sister Seven,” Hu explained when she did. “I know machine-reeled silk is at least two grades better. But a man must have settled principles and keep faith. I have always opposed modern reeling. If I now undertake it myself, will I not be an unprincipled turncoat? What could I say when challenged?” “Times change. The world changes every day. I grew up in Shanghai: how could I ever have imagined the city as it is now? Everything advances in the West. Refuse the benefits, and you suffer. Could His Excellency Zuo have succeeded in the west without the foreign weapons you bought him?” “You misunderstand. I do not say the silk is inferior—” “I know, and I have not misunderstood. My point is that one must recognize the direction of events. Fall behind, and by the time you try to catch up it is too late. You have helped Yingchun so greatly; our concern for you is entirely sincere.” “Sister Seven!” Hu interrupted hastily. “To speak like that makes our friendship sound shallow.”

“Very well. But I truly am anxious for you. The situation is bad. I hear the French intend to station warships off Wusong and stop merchant vessels passing. If the export trade stops, what becomes of the cocoons and silk in which you have invested several million taels?” “Where did you hear that?” “From the foreign doctor treating me.” “Really?” “When have I ever lied to you?” “Of course—my mistake,” Hu apologized hastily. “There are times to take risks and times to be cautious. Forgive a coarse saying used by men about town: ‘Once you possess even a ragged quilt, you must protect what you have.’ How much have you to protect now, Little Uncle?”

After a long silence, Hu sighed. “Do you think I do not know? But sometimes the choice is not mine.” “I do not believe that. You built this business yourself. No one else can decide for you.” “You do not quite understand. There are many unseen restraints. If I start operating modern mills, people will come begging: ‘Instead of helping us, Mr Hu, you kick us from behind. We silk-reeling families will have nothing to eat.’ Then I cannot harden my heart.” The Seventh Lady had not expected him to forestall her argument so effectively. But she would not give up; she must find another way to persuade him. “That is like climbing into a well to rescue someone. Is it worth risking yourself? Modern reeling is the way things are moving. At present its silk goes only to the foreign market. As mills multiply, everyone will prefer cloth woven from machine-reeled silk, and no one will want the traditional product. Look at cotton cloth. Foreign cloth is fine, white, and light. Who does not want a shirt of it in summer? Only country people still wear coarse blue homespun, and in a few years even they will stop.” “You cannot apply one rule to everything.” “Why not? The principle is the same. With a rescue from a well, your own risk is one question; whether the person can usefully be saved is another. If a healthy, vigorous man drowns, that is a pity. But if he is a consumptive, skin and bones, he will have few years left even if rescued. Frankly, it makes little difference. Traditional silk-reeling is like that dying consumptive.” There seemed something in her comparison. Hu decided that argument alone was useless; he had better confine himself to his own difficulty.

“A man cannot be double-faced, playing both the spirit-medium and the ghost. If I behaved like that, I should certainly lose all I have.” Unable to refute him, the Seventh Lady turned the problem anxiously over in her mind. Suddenly she had an inspiration. “Then you do want modern mills, but those restraints prevent you. Is that right?” “Yes.” “Without the restraints, you could proceed?” “You could put it that way.” “Then I can guarantee a way to remove them.” “Let me hear it.” “Simple. Keep your own name out of it.” “You mean… be the owner behind the scenes?” “Exactly.”

Hu was tempted, but the matter was too important to decide without thought. Seeing the possibility of a change, the Seventh Lady knew she must keep pressing. “The situation can deteriorate very quickly. If you do not prepare now, there may be no remedy when you discover the danger. Several million taels is no small sum. Even the God of Wealth could scarcely bear that risk.” Hu could not disregard this. “Who would undertake it?” Once they were discussing a representative, the problem became easier. “I should like Madam Luosi to come,” she said. “My body is paralysed, but my mind is sound. I could help her think things through.” “And who would manage the household? It might work if you could get about.” “I certainly cannot. Perhaps…” She hesitated. “Yingchun? Everyone knows his connection with me. His appearance would be much the same as mine. Such self-deception would hardly do.” “I was not thinking of him, only wondering where to find someone trustworthy.” She paused. “Think of someone yourself first. If there really is no one, I have a person in mind.” “Tell me.” “No. You must consider your own people first.”

Hu reflected that Gu’s participation would be indispensable, yet Gu could not appear openly. If the Seventh Lady nominated someone, it would be almost as reassuring as having Gu act himself. He therefore did not even consider his own people. “I have no one, Sister Seven. If you do, we can discuss it further with greater confidence.” He was making her take responsibility for the recommendation. She understood and reconsidered carefully, satisfying herself that nothing should go wrong before speaking. “Let my fifth brother act for us.” Brother You had long been retired, and few in Shanghai’s business world knew him. His influence in the Grain Transport Brotherhood remained, however. With Gu’s assistance, he could undertake the task. “If Brother Five agrees, I have no objection,” Hu said. “We shall discuss it properly when Yingchun returns.”

Gu made a special journey to Songjiang to bring Brother You back for a meeting. Hu insisted on one condition: everything must be kept secret. He would remain entirely behind the scenes to avoid unnecessary trouble. “If you want nobody to know, the only sure course is not to do it,” Brother You said frankly. “But once the concern is established and the rice cooked, it will not matter if people discover it.” “True enough. Still, I should like something to say by way of an excuse.” “If you deny it, what can they do?” the Seventh Lady asked. “Go to Beijing for a while then, and spare yourself even the noise.” “Exactly. I shall keep out of the way.” Hu had thus fully accepted the couple’s advice on this large undertaking. Silk-reeling and cocoon purchases ranked third among his businesses, behind banking and pawnbroking. The banks and pawnshops, however, had affiliated houses and carried on regular operations, each with a principal manager in overall charge. Hu alone directed the silk and cocoon trade, drawing upon banking and pawnshop staff whenever necessary. Advances for purchasing the future crop required Fukang’s senior clerks in Huzhou and elsewhere; when silk and cocoon warehouses lacked space, the pawnshops had to assist; foreign sales inevitably called for Gu’s services. The head clerks in individual silk and cocoon firms managed only their own portions of the business. Their rank was far below that of chief managers such as Mi. For years Hu had sought someone capable of overseeing the whole concern, to whom he could entrust it completely. Now Gu seemed the natural choice.

“I am doing as you advise, Yingchun, and this business will henceforth be conducted differently. I therefore entrust all the silk and cocoon operations to you. Nothing hinders business more than having one’s hands tied. Whether cooperating or competing with foreigners, one must have prompt information and make immediate decisions. Without full authority, if you still have to consult me at a critical moment, you will inevitably lose a move.” His reasoning was clear and his sincerity moving. But Gu felt the responsibility too heavy to accept. The Seventh Lady remained silent, apparently sharing his concern, which made him more cautious still. Yet he dared not refuse. To insist on declining would imply that he had no confidence in modern reeling. What motive, then, had he had for urging someone else into it? That alone would be impossible to explain.

“I am grateful for your confidence, Little Uncle. After so many years of friendship, I have no reason to refuse. But this is a business turning over several million a year, with very wide connections. Knowing only its surface, I dare not assume such responsibility before understanding it thoroughly.” “Naturally. But you are to steer the ship; others will do the work below. Most of the specialists have served me for years. I shall bring them together to explain things. In a day or two, you will understand the essentials.” “If I take over, that will certainly be necessary.” Gu adroitly deferred the point. “We must proceed step by step. Let me first help Brother Five complete the purchase of the two mills. Then we can discuss the next stage.” “That is the proper course,” the Seventh Lady agreed. “The busy silkworm season is over for this year. Apart from the modern mills, everything can follow the established arrangements. The immediate priority is to sell the stock you hold.” Her last sentence mattered most. Still fearing a worsening political situation and declining trade, she considered conversion of goods into cash the safest course. Hu thought precisely the opposite. With modern mills of his own, he need not fear finding an outlet for his cocoons. Secure on that point, why not fight the foreign merchants without restraint? Before a transaction, Hu consulted widely and willingly sought advice from anyone. At the moment of decision, however, he made up his own mind. That was his nature; more than ten years of Zuo’s influence had also taught him the meaning of Yue Fei’s saying that the art of applying a method rests in the commander’s own judgment. He gave the Seventh Lady’s suggestion little thought and merely nodded to show he had heard. “Let us turn to the immediate arrangements. Since Brother Five is to represent the concern, we must make it convincing. I think two contracts are needed.” “Certainly,” Brother You said. “I should like to see how much work and responsibility I am taking on. Only a contract will make that clear.” “You misunderstand me. I am not asking you to bear responsibility. With you representing us and Yingchun involved, you need not carry the burden. If the mills prosper, may they bring you good fortune in your later years, and us some reflected benefit.” “You have that backwards, Little Uncle—” “Enough politeness!” the Seventh Lady interrupted impatiently. “Tell us about the two contracts.” “First, calculate the purchase price of the mills, the cost of setting them up, and the working capital needed after they open. With that total, arrange a credit agreement with Fukang and fix a limit. Within it, funds can be drawn against the passbook at any time. I shall tell Mi to make the details especially convenient.” “Please ask Mi to draft the agreement,” Gu said. “Once I have negotiated and calculated the amount, I shall report it. What is the second contract?” “For raw materials. Calculate the quantity of cocoons required, then draw up an agreement to buy them from me on credit, specifying when payment is due. Keep it simple. Above all, it must contain no mention of modern silk mills. As far as I am concerned, this is a cocoon firm. You buy cocoons from me; you need not tell me what you do with them, and I have no right to ask. Do you understand?”

“Of course.” Gu looked at Brother You. “In a word, Little Uncle’s name must not be connected with modern reeling mills.” “Then we must first obtain a ministry licence and establish a cocoon firm.” “Exactly,” Hu agreed. “That will put everything in proper order.” “I shall arrange it. Any other instructions?” “Nothing more from me. Have you anything else to discuss?” “Nothing occurs to me now. I shall ask when it does.” “We must not speak only of the mills,” the Seventh Lady interposed. “That silk must not be held any longer.” “Yes,” Gu said. “It should be sold when a good price is available.” “Certainly.” Hu’s agreement reassured her. Gu, however, knew that “good” meant different things to different people. Some considered merely recovering their capital a good price; others rejected a price because the profit was insufficient. Hu’s good price was certainly not the Seventh Lady’s.

During their conversation, a messenger from the Transport Bureau arrived. Zuo had sent a special courier with a letter marked “Deliver within two days” and bearing the great purple seal of the governor-general of Liangjiang. Its urgency was evident before it was opened. Hu read it, reflected briefly, and rose. “Come with me to Jixian Lane, Yingchun.” He meant Fukang Bank. Mi was out on business. The second manager, Zhou Xiaotang, who supervised the general accounts, sent apprentices in several directions to find him. Meanwhile, he ushered Hu into a lavishly furnished private room opened only for his visits and attended to him with great solicitude. “Attend to your work, Xiaotang. Mr Gu and I must speak.” Once Zhou had withdrawn with repeated acknowledgments, Hu showed Gu the letter. Shandong had suffered floods that year. Great breaches had opened in embankments at Qihe, Licheng, Qidong, and other places along tributaries of the Yellow River. Muddy water poured through, causing severe devastation. Governor Chen Shijie had obtained approval to provide relief through work: the victims would repair the embankments and receive wages instead of charity. Labour and materials would be costly. Besides a substantial grant from the central treasury, several prosperous provinces had been assessed contributions. Liangjiang’s share was four hundred thousand taels, but the Jiangning provincial treasury could raise only half. With no alternative, Zuo appealed to Hu. “Shandong’s river calamity is grave. The court has ordered relief, and the authorities intend to provide it through public works. Could you lend me two hundred thousand?”

“Indeed!” Gu exclaimed with feeling. “Liangjiang’s wealth is known throughout the country, yet it cannot raise even four hundred thousand taels. Imagine the state’s poverty.” “Who knows when this two hundred thousand can be repaid?” Hu said. “I may as well make it a contribution.” “No! This is no time for conspicuous generosity.” “Why not?” “First, there has been no imperial appeal for donations. Why volunteer, as though you had more money than you could use? Second, the market is poor. A gift of two hundred thousand would make everyone jealous. Third, defence needs are urgent and military expenditure immense. If someone proposed asking wealthy merchants for contributions, you would be the first approached. Then two hundred thousand might be far from enough. You must not draw that attention to yourself!” The last warning was blunt, and Hu could not disregard it. “Very well. Draft a telegram asking where payment is required.” Gu wrote: “Governor-General’s Office, Jiangning. Confidential. Your letter received. Your instructions will be carried out. Please advise whether payment is to be made in Jiangning or Jinan. Respectfully, your subordinate Circuit Intendant Hu Guangyong.” Hu read it and inserted “immediately” into the request for instructions, then gave it to Zhou to send to the Transport Bureau for transmission.

Mi had meanwhile been found. “Has HSBC transferred the five hundred thousand?” Hu asked. “Yes.” “Untouched?” “Entirely. But a quarter-year’s interest was deducted first, so it is no longer the full amount.” “I know. I have allocated the funds. His Excellency Zuo is borrowing two hundred thousand; the remainder is to be lent to a cocoon firm.” Neither use had occurred to Mi. He had meant to earn short-term interest through a small bank owned by his younger cousin, underreporting the return and pocketing the difference. Disappointed at the collapse of this pleasant calculation, he nevertheless hoped to salvage something.

“What does His Excellency need the money for? What requires two hundred thousand?” “The borrower is not he personally, but the Jiangning provincial treasury.” A personal loan to Zuo might not all be required at once, leaving room to use the money briefly. A public loan offered no such hope. “And the cocoon firm? Which is it? What is its name?” “I do not know its name yet.” Mi grew still more astonished. “You are lending so much without even knowing the firm’s name?” “It is not really a loan to someone else. We shall use it ourselves. You must draft a contract.” Hu turned to Gu. “Explain the arrangements to Mi. Once the draft is ready and the contracts signed, I can prepare to return to Hangzhou.” Mi listened in silence as Gu described the proposed mill purchase. He was deeply displeased. He considered himself Hu’s foremost chief manager, senior even to Tang Zishao. Silk was closely connected with the banking business. To have been excluded beforehand from so important a decision filled him with jealousy and resentment. “Just wait,” he thought. “Tip over the dog’s feeding bowl, and no one gets to eat.”

CHAPTER SEVEN

The contracts were drafted. But a cocoon firm could not open without approval from the Ministry of Revenue, and Mi used this as a pretext to suggest waiting for the ministry licence before signing. Hu and Gu had no suspicion of his malicious intentions. To them the contracts were merely a formality: once the mill purchase was agreed, the money would be available whenever required. They therefore consented. With his Shanghai business concluded, Hu hurried back to Hangzhou through the oppressive summer heat. A marriage had been proposed for his third daughter, and his mother had accepted on her behalf: she was to marry the only son of “Wang the Philanthropist.” Wang, whose personal name was Caisheng, had been Hu’s friend for many years. In their youth both had been shop assistants, the men Hangzhou people jokingly called “counter monkeys,” though in different trades. Wang had begun as an apprentice in a large sauce-making establishment. When Hu renewed his acquaintance with Wang Youling and rose rapidly in the world, Wang Caisheng remained an assistant. After the Taiping rebellion was suppressed, however, he reappeared in the guise of a gentleman. Some said he had enriched himself by looting during the disorder; others that he had unearthed gold, silver, and jewels buried by the rebels. Whatever the source of his fortune, one fact was certain: at Hu’s invitation he helped with reconstruction, tending the wounded, saving the dying, and aiding displaced people. These good works earned him the title “Philanthropist.” In the second year after Hangzhou’s recovery, a son was born to him, and people called it a reward for his charity.

That was the yichou year, the fourth of Tongzhi. The boy, nicknamed A’niu, was now nineteen. Wang had long wanted a marriage connection with Hu, but Hu thought A’niu dull—indeed, as stupid as the ox suggested by his name—and had consistently refused. Unexpectedly, the young man had now passed into the county school as a licentiate, and the proposal was renewed. The matchmaker argued that his honest, kindly nature would ensure that Hu’s daughter was never mistreated. As an only son’s wife, she would also be cherished by her parents-in-law. Wang’s wealth fell far short of Hu’s, but his reputation for benevolence made up the difference; the families could be considered suitable equals. The sole deficiency had been A’niu’s lack of a degree. Now a licentiate, he had a promising future: as the saying went, “A licentiate is the seedling of a chief minister.” It was an excellent match. The argument was reasonable. Hu’s mother particularly valued the fact that, with no brothers-in-law’s wives in the household, her granddaughter would suffer no friction from sisters-in-law. She therefore accepted, merely writing to inform Hu and urge him home: the formal betrothal ceremony was set for the seventh day of the seventh month. On returning, he learned that the wedding too had been fixed, for the fifth day of the eleventh month. Wang’s aged mother was frail and might die at any time; she longed to see her grandson’s bride enter the household. If she died first, the wedding would have to wait three years, an excessive delay. The reasoning was entirely proper, and Hu’s mother agreed. Fortunately the dowry had long been prepared. Only some fashionable foreign goods remained to be added.

The scale of the celebration, however, awaited Hu’s return. Four people had to decide: Hu, his mother, his wife, and his concubine, Madam Luosi. All four had conflicting inclinations. The three women favoured restraint, yet Hu’s mother loved this youngest granddaughter best and feared slighting her. His wife wanted equal treatment: the girl should enjoy the same splendour as her two elder sisters. Madam Luosi thought of her own daughter. A restrained wedding now would set a precedent limiting the display when her daughter married. Hu naturally preferred the grandest possible occasion, but feared criticism in a depressed market. Two days of discussion produced no decision. At last he concluded, “The occasion must be respectable. The difference between a large and small celebration is limited anyway. There are still four months; we shall see how matters stand.” “The celebration is for other people to look at,” Madam Luosi observed. “The dowry brings real benefit. Give Third Miss a splendid dowry, and a smaller celebration will do. Publicly, we can explain that business is poor; privately, she will not feel slighted. Even the bridegroom’s family can have no objection.” This considered every side. Both Hu’s mother and his wife were pleased, while Hu felt that a magnificent dowry would preserve his dignity even without a lavish ceremony.

“Sister Luo is right. We must not stint the dowry. But the additions will have to be bought ready-made.” “Then someone must go to Shanghai,” Hu’s wife said after her mother-in-law, looking at Madam Luosi. Madam Luosi wanted to volunteer, but reconsidered and remained silent. There were too many tongues in the household. However hard she tried, someone would talk behind her back, perhaps alleging that she begrudged the expense because Third Miss was not her own daughter. Hu had expected her to respond. When she did not, he decided for her. “You know Shanghai. Go there, and visit the Seventh Lady while you are about it.” “Of course I should go for Third Miss’s wedding. But expensive jewellery is not necessarily pleasing. Naturally I would choose costly pieces, yet their designs might not suit her taste.” She smiled. “Why not let me take her and have her choose for herself at the foreign firms and jewellers?” “Quite improper!” the Old Madam exclaimed in broad Hangzhou speech. “An unmarried girl choosing her own dowry! People would laugh themselves sick if they heard of it.” “You go yourself,” Hu repeated.

Madam Luosi still would not commit herself. “I wonder whether Third Miss might enjoy a visit,” she murmured, as if to herself. “There is no need,” Hu’s wife said. “Surely you know what jewellery she likes.” Finally the Old Madam settled the matter: Madam Luosi would go alone. She would, of course, first consult the girl’s preferences and Hu’s wife’s wishes. Most important was a spending limit, which only Hu could set. “The dowry has already cost over a hundred thousand taels. Spend no more than another fifty thousand on additions. Money is tight in Shanghai, so goods must be cheap. Fifty thousand should buy at least seventy thousand’s worth.”

In Shanghai, Gu accompanied her to the German firm Biefa. Their first inquiries showed that the opposite was true. Domestic products were indeed being discounted for ready cash, but imported goods had risen in price. “The situation grows more tense every day, Mr Gu,” the manager explained. “France has a new premier, Jules Ferry, a very determined man. If China refuses to yield over Vietnam, he means to go to war. Ever since the foreign newspapers reported Admiral Courbet’s arrival in Vietnam, goods at every foreign firm have risen by ten to fifteen per cent. Some things cannot now be bought at any price.” “Why?” Madam Luosi asked. “If war begins, French warships will close China’s ports and foreign merchant ships will be unable to enter. Once supplies stop, sellers can ask whatever they please. The question will be whether an article is available, not whether it is dear. Every item sold now is one less for later, so everyone is putting the best goods aside.” “No wonder!” She pointed at the jewellery in the glass cabinets. “There is nothing here I like.” “Your taste is naturally exceptional, Madam Hu. We would not risk offending an old customer. Tell me what you require, and I shall open the safe for you.” Madam Luosi knew that foreigners in China, whatever their nationality, acted together. They too had agreements among traders. One firm would be much like another, so the maxim about comparing three shops would be little use. Better to negotiate as a major customer.

“To be frank, I am buying a dowry for our third daughter. If we agree, I shall give you the entire order—several tens of thousands of taels. Otherwise, Biefa is hardly Shanghai’s only large foreign firm.” The prospect demanded attention. “Third Miss’s dowry must be treated seriously. Please come inside. If you have a list, let me take it and assemble the goods for you to examine.” She had prepared a list but would not reveal her hand. “No list. If the goods are good and the prices reasonable, I shall buy more. First show me two pairs of diamond bracelets.” Chinese and Western fashions differed. To Chinese customers, diamond bracelets were the most precious jewellery. The request confirmed that she really was buying a dowry and that the order might indeed run to tens of thousands. The manager became still more attentive. He escorted her and Gu to the foreign director’s private sitting room and specially brought in a foreign saleswoman who spoke Chinese. Her name was Aidun; Madam Luosi called her “Miss Ai.”

“Where are you from, Miss Ai?” “I was born in Edinburgh,” she replied while preparing tea with milk. Madam Luosi did not know the place, so Gu explained that she was British. “Then your country is like ours,” Madam Luosi said. “Both are ruled by an old empress dowager.” Aidun’s Chinese covered everyday conversation only. Talk of an “empress dowager ruling” left her as bewildered as the name Edinburgh had left Madam Luosi. Gu had to explain again. “She means your Queen Victoria and our Empress Dowager Cixi.” Aidun was surprised. She had served many Chinese women—courtesans, and occasionally ladies of rank or respectable young women—but none had mentioned the British queen. She warmed to Madam Luosi and served her afternoon tea with particular care. The manager then brought three oblong boxes, all of black leather with gold lettering. Inside the first, a pair of brilliantly sparkling platinum bracelets set with diamonds rested on blue velvet. The workmanship was exquisite.

On closer inspection, however, the box looked new while the metal’s colour seemed otherwise. “These are used?” she asked. “Yes. They were favourite jewels of Napoleon’s empress.” “I care nothing for empresses. New things are better for a dowry.” “Both these other pairs are new.” One was set entirely with diamonds; the other combined diamonds with rubies and sapphires. The all-diamond pair was the more valuable. Each bracelet held four diamonds the size of soybeans, linked by smaller stones, and glittered even when held still. For splendour, however, the coloured stones excelled. “What are the prices?” “Thirty-five thousand for these; thirty-two thousand for the pair with coloured stones. I advise the all-diamond pair, Madam Hu. It costs three thousand more but is better value.” Unable to decide, she asked Aidun to put them on.

Aidun wore one kind on each wrist and held out her arms for inspection. After a long look, Madam Luosi turned to Gu. “What do you think, Brother-in-law?” “The all-diamond pair is better, certainly, but rather too plain in colour.” This exactly matched her own view, and she decided at once. “For a bride, with the Old Madam present, the effect should not be too subdued.” She turned to the manager. “I have chosen. Now we must discuss the price, or choosing is pointless. When did these bracelets arrive?” “More than a year ago.” “What price did you mark them at then?” “Thirty thousand.” “I do not believe it. You have added only two thousand—less than ten per cent.” “It is true.” He drew a label from beneath the velvet lining. “Mr Gu, please look.” The Arabic numerals did indeed read thirty thousand. Madam Luosi could read them too and responded quickly. “You yourself said the all-diamond pair was better value, so this one must be poor value. You must have marked an excessive price originally and felt too guilty to raise it by even ten per cent. Is that not so?” The manager smiled ruefully. “You are formidable, Madam Hu. I have no answer.”

She smiled. “However reasonable our arguments, too much disputing spoils goodwill. Let us do this: I shall certainly buy the bracelets from you. Show me the other goods first, and we shall settle their price last.” He agreed. They examined crystal plates and bowls, perfumes, and all manner of ingenious ornaments. Hoping to obtain a good price for the gem-set bracelets, the manager priced these other articles especially fairly. Once her choices were complete, they returned to the bracelets. “These goods total twelve thousand,” she said. “My husband told me not to spend more than forty thousand on additions to the dowry. What do you say? No bargaining now—yes or no.” “Madam Hu, how can I withstand such a crushing stroke?” “Business cannot be forced. If we cannot agree on the bracelets, I must look elsewhere. The twelve thousand is settled; I shall draw you a draft for that now.” Stunned, the manager signalled Aidun to serve more tea and refreshments, then quietly drew Gu aside. “I truly admire her methods,” he said with a bitter smile. “To accommodate her, I offered those later goods at cost. Intendant Sheng offered three thousand for one of the large crystal chandeliers, and we refused; I have charged her only two thousand five hundred. If she does not buy the bracelets, this transaction will cost me my job.” “She is willing to buy. You are unwilling to sell.”

“It is not that I am unwilling. The price is wrong.” “Profit is secondary here. Even without it, you gain. Consider the value of people saying that Biefa supplied the splendid dowry when Hu, the God of Wealth, married off his daughter.” “That reputation is precisely why I have been so accommodating. But I really cannot conclude the business at forty thousand.” “Would you lose money?” “Not exactly. But future prices—” “The future is the future; this is now. Frankly, trade is poor, and wealthy people are already fleeing the danger. You may not see another order like this later.” After a long silence, the manager said, “If I accept, I shall sacrifice my entire bonus.” Gu knew how foreign firms worked: salaries were modest, and employees depended on sales bonuses. The man seemed truthful. Madam Luosi had plainly bargained very hard—which also confirmed that she was obtaining excellent value. Some concession was needed lest they lose the opportunity.

“In that case, I shall help you,” Gu said, lowering his voice. “I shall take responsibility for having Madam Hu give you five hundred taels privately to compensate for your loss.” The manager wanted more. But Gu had presented this as a favour; refusing would look ungrateful, lose the sale, and offend a major customer. That was hardly sound business. He had no real choice. “Thank you, Mr Gu. The director happens to be here. I shall explain the position to him. Since you can decide for Madam Hu, there is no need to tell her of your promise just yet.” Gu understood. The manager feared that she might carelessly let something slip. To the foreign owners, accepting a private payment from a customer amounted to dishonesty. Discovery could mean not only dismissal but prosecution. Gu nodded emphatically to show that he fully understood. The manager excused himself to Madam Luosi and went to see the director. Soon he returned with a blue-eyed foreigner, square-jawed and wearing an upward-curving moustache. Gu recognized him at once as German.

The newcomer was indeed Biefa’s manager, Weilianshi. He spoke no English, while Gu knew no German, so the head clerk had to interpret. Introductions and courteous greetings followed. Weilianshi said that he had long admired Hu’s reputation and felt deeply honored that Biefa should supply the dowry for his beloved daughter’s marriage. Whether the price fell below cost was hardly worth considering. He would accept Madam Luosi’s offer and, in addition, have a silver salver specially made as a wedding gift. Delighted, she could not resist turning to Gu with a smile. “Such good fortune! I never expected it.” “Do not rejoice too soon, Sister Luo,” he answered. “I think there is something more.” “Mr Gu sees very clearly,” the clerk said. “Our manager has a proposal for Madam Hu. He would like to display the Third Miss’s dowry here for a month. Afterward, we would send a special escort to deliver it in Hangzhou.” Halfway through this explanation, Gu had given Madam Luosi a warning glance. She therefore listened quietly, neither agreeing nor refusing, and left him to answer. “How would you display it?”

“We would set aside half the shop and cordon it off with red silk ropes.” “Would there be an explanation?” “Naturally. It is something that brings distinction to everyone.” “Certainly. But we must discuss it. Is this a condition of the sale?” The clerk looked surprised. He had never imagined that the buyers could object. “Would displaying it cause some inconvenience, Mr Gu?” “Possibly. There is no need to explain why just now. Nor can we yet decide whether to agree. Please ask your manager only this: if we decline the display, does the sale fall through?” The clerk nodded and spoke with his employer in German for some time. “He says this is an additional request, not a condition of the sale. But we sincerely hope you will do us the honor.” “It is not my decision,” Gu explained quickly. “As you say, the display would bring credit to everyone, and I too would like to see it. But Mr Hu is a senior servant of the court. His public reputation matters. If we cannot meet your manager’s wishes, we must ask his understanding.” At the mention of an official’s reputation, the clerk understood and nodded repeatedly. “Of course. When might we hear your answer?”

After considering, Gu said, “Prepare a list of everything we saw today, with the prices, and come to my house tomorrow morning to discuss payment. I may be able to answer about the display then. If we must write to Hangzhou, however, it will take another half month.” “Certainly. I shall call on you myself tomorrow.” Madam Luosi was extremely pleased with the day’s visit to Biefa. Sitting in the armchair beside the Seventh Lady’s bed, she described it with lively words and gestures. When Gu mentioned privately promising the clerk five hundred taels in compensation, she not only accepted the expense but praised his handling of it. Naturally, the Seventh Lady was pleased too. “There is one other matter,” Madam Luosi said. “Let my brother-in-law explain.” “It needs discussion, not merely explanation.” Gu described the proposed display and asked their opinion. “I see no objection,” Madam Luosi said. “What do you think, Seventh Sister?” “It might be too ostentatious.” “Especially now,” Gu added, “with floods in Shandong and the general situation unsettled. People may criticize.” Madam Luosi fell silent. She glanced at the Seventh Lady, and her face darkened.

“Yingchun,” the Seventh Lady said, giving him a look, “telephone the doctor for me. Tell him my mixture is finished and ask for two more doses.” Understanding, Gu nodded and went out, leaving the women free to speak privately. “Seventh Sister,” Madam Luosi said without disguising her wish, “I truly want these additions to our Third Miss’s dowry displayed for everyone to see.” The Seventh Lady had not imagined that she cared so deeply and seriously about it. For a moment she could say nothing. Once Madam Luosi expressed an opinion, she always carried it through thoroughly. “If that German had not suggested it, the idea would never have occurred to me. The moment he spoke, I wanted it. Think of all the work that goes into marrying off a daughter. What is it for? To make a splendid occasion. When the dowry goes out, everyone should come to look. The more people, the greater the honor. After all the care and effort, to hear praise and envy on that one day makes one think: ‘Ah, this is what it means to live!’ Remember how we felt as girls, watching wealthy families marry their daughters. Think of Mr Hu’s feelings now. Would the German’s proposal not tempt him?”

The Seventh Lady’s thoughts began to follow the same course. In rich and distinguished families, the grandest celebrations were parents’ birthdays and daughters’ marriages. Yet after sixty came seventy, and after seventy, eighty or ninety: there would be further opportunities for splendid birthdays. A daughter’s wedding came only once. Parents had only that occasion on which to show their love in full, and could therefore add magnificence to magnificence without limit. As a child, the Seventh Lady had seen one great household’s dowry procession end with eight middle-aged men in new dark-blue robes. Each carried a red-lacquered tray holding a thick ledger bound in coarse blue cloth. What sort of dowry was that? On asking, she learned that the bride was receiving eight pawnshops. The eight men were their senior appraisers, and the books their general ledgers. Such an extraordinary dowry displayed wealth so impressively that the memory never faded. Now Biefa proposed to exhibit part of the Third Miss’s dowry in Shanghai, where people from every region gathered. News of it could carry stories of Hu’s lavish wedding preparations to Yunnan, Guizhou, Sichuan, and the remotest places. No amount of money could buy that publicity. Small wonder Madam Luosi was tempted. “What Mr Hu loves above all is prestige. If I let such an opportunity pass, I should feel I had failed him. Do you not agree?”

“Then why not ask him?” the Seventh Lady suggested. “We cannot. If we ask—” Madam Luosi paused. “Put yourself in his place.” A moment’s thought showed the difficulty. A man fond of outward show would never admit it. Asked directly, he would say, “Never mind, never mind,” in a tone neither eager nor actually opposed. Yet circumstances were different now; perhaps they could discuss the matter candidly with him.

Before the Seventh Lady settled that thought, Madam Luosi spoke again. “To tell the truth, buying these additions to the dowry is a thankless task. There is no fixed standard for prices or quality. No one will notice a bargain, but let me overpay for one thing and there will be plenty of talk behind my back. If Biefa displays what I have bought, that proves its worth, and no one will dare complain. I can account for myself to our old lady, the Third Miss’s mother, and everyone in the Hu household. I want them to know that I care for her even more than if she were my own daughter.” The last words moved the Seventh Lady. This mattered deeply to Madam Luosi’s reputation and standing in the family. The exhibition would prove both the excellence of her purchases and her generosity toward a daughter she loved as her own. Conversely, if such an opportunity for distinction were refused, no one would understand that the reason was to avoid ostentation harmful to Hu’s official reputation. They would say the goods were worthless and too embarrassing to show. The difference between these impressions was crucial. After pondering for some time, the Seventh Lady said, “Biefa’s displays are for foreigners. Few Chinese enter foreign shops, so it makes little difference to them whether the goods are displayed. These things would chiefly be shown to foreigners. In that case, I have an idea. See what you think.”

“Tell me.” “Let them exhibit the goods, but require every description to be in English. No Chinese characters. That way it will look less ostentatious.” After a moment Madam Luosi answered with an emphatic “Good,” seeming entirely willing to follow her lead. The Seventh Lady then called, “Little Sister!” For several months she had addressed Ruixiang that way, and Ruixiang had accepted it naturally, answering readily. With Madam Luosi present, however, she grew shy and did not even dare reply aloud. She merely hurried to the bedside to await instructions.

“Find out where the master is and ask him to come.” Ruixiang went. Madam Luosi lowered her voice. “I came for three things: the Third Miss’s dowry, a visit to you in your illness, and Ruixiang. Why have they still not begun married life together?” “I have urged him several times.” “Him” meant Gu, who had now appeared at the door. The Seventh Lady fell silent but gestured and gave Madam Luosi a look indicating that they would discuss it later. “Yingchun, I have thought of a way, and Sister Luo agrees.” She explained the plan. Gu thought it scarcely better than stopping one’s ears while stealing a bell. Still, it was preferable to Chinese labels. He nodded. “I shall tell Biefa’s clerk when he comes. But—”

He left the sentence unfinished, but the Seventh Lady understood. “Provided it stays out of the newspapers, it cannot attract too much attention. Are you not a friend of the ‘Rebel First Scholar’—the man you go drinking with in the pleasure quarters?” “Yes! You have reminded me. I shall speak to him. Is there anything else?” “Only that.” Gu turned to Madam Luosi. “I am sorry I cannot dine with you tonight, Sister Luo. I have an important appointment with Mi Benchang and must leave now. Oh, he knows you are here and wants to invite you to dinner. When would you be free?” “No, thank him for me. He lives in Shanghai without his family; it would be awkward for me to visit. Please decline firmly on my behalf.” Once Gu had gone, Madam Luosi hastened to ask about the “Rebel First Scholar.” “His surname is Wang, and his name Tao—the same character as in Hangzhou’s Taoguang,” the Seventh Lady explained. “When the rebels were powerful, they held examinations, and Wang Tao came first. Everyone in Shanghai calls him the ‘Rebel First Scholar.’” “What does that have to do with the newspapers?” “He works at Shenbao and has considerable influence there. If Yingchun asks him to keep the dowry display out of the paper, the family will not hear about it, and there will be no trouble.” “I see.” Madam Luosi glanced toward Ruixiang.

The Seventh Lady understood at once and sent Ruixiang to supervise the kitchen, freeing them to discuss her. “I have urged Yingchun several times. He always says we can talk later. Business is poor, he says, and he has no heart for such matters. Your visit is timely. Persuade him for me. If he still refuses, take it up with him.” “Take it up with him?” Madam Luosi was astonished. “How could I approach my brother-in-law on a matter like that?” “Why not? Ruixiang came from you. You ought to speak for her.” “Ah!” Madam Luosi laughed. “Once a thing passes through your mouth, even the unreasonable becomes reasonable.” “It is reasonable.” The Seventh Lady lowered her voice. “They are all right: one is in no hurry, and the other is probably impatient inwardly but says nothing. I am the one who suffers, feeling as though I owe Ruixiang something.” “With that reason, I certainly can take it up with him. He will have to choose a day.” “If we wait for him, he will find more excuses. Let us choose it ourselves. There must at least be a wedding cup of wine, and you must stay to drink it.” “Of course.” Madam Luosi considered. “Today is the twenty-eighth of the eighth month. This is a short month; the ninth begins the day after tomorrow. The Third Miss’s wedding is only two months away. I really am longing to get home.” “I know, I know. The almanac hangs behind the dressing-table mirror. Please bring it.” They consulted it and found the third day of the ninth month auspicious for everything. Madam Luosi’s eagerness to return to Hangzhou left no time to choose another date. They settled at once on that day for Gu and Ruixiang to begin their married life together.

“She must have some new clothes and a couple of pieces of jewelry,” Madam Luosi said. “Those will be my dowry gift. You need not trouble about them.” “Your gift is yours,” the Seventh Lady replied. “I have prepared something too, though perhaps not enough. Do not stand on ceremony.” Reaching beneath her pillow, she took out a Fukang passbook. “Take her shopping tomorrow. Whatever she likes, please buy it for her on my behalf.” Their friendship allowed neither polite hesitation nor refusal. Madam Luosi took the passbook and put it into her pocket without looking inside. “Our old lady worries about you so much. Will you be able to come to the wedding in the eleventh month?” “I should like to. But I may have difficulty getting about and cause you trouble.” “What trouble? We need only assign another couple of maids or older serving women to look after you. And Ruixiang will be there.” Long confined to bed, the Seventh Lady had often wished to go somewhere, and the invitation made her still more eager. Her chief concern was that a household already overwhelmed with wedding preparations might lack time to care for her properly. They would feel uneasy, and she herself might not be comfortable either. Despite Madam Luosi’s encouragement, she still felt the matter required thought.

“Madam,” Ruixiang said, coming in, “both dishes you mentioned yesterday have arrived. Are you hungry? If so, I shall serve dinner.” “Which dishes?” The previous evening Madam Luosi had fondly recalled several foods she used to enjoy and could not tell which two Ruixiang meant. “Did you not say you missed zaobotou, the wine-lees casserole, and dumplings stuffed with greens most of all?” “Exactly! But they must come from Sanpailou and Tao A’da’s.” “They do. I gave particular instructions to buy from those places. The casserole may be too rich for my mistress, but she can eat the vegetable dumplings. Shall I bring dinner in here?” “Yes, yes!” The Seventh Lady loved company. “I grew up in Shanghai and have always heard of Sanpailou’s vegetable dumplings, but never seen them. I must try them tonight.” “Several shops there make them. Widow Xu’s are the best.” “What is so good about them?” In Shanghai, the glutinous-rice dumplings eaten at the Lantern Festival were called yuanzi. Widow Xu’s, at Sanpailou, gave full value. Frugal customers would buy a bowl containing four meat dumplings, eat only the wrappers, and take the four balls of filling home to stew with cabbage and bean-thread noodles for another meal. Her greatest specialty, however, was the vegetable variety. “She says she has a secret, but once explained it is nothing remarkable,” Madam Luosi said. “I ate there several times and watched quietly until I understood. The secret is painstaking work. Only the finest leaves are chosen; yellow and old ones are discarded. Even the tender leaves have every vein stripped out. They are briefly blanched, chopped very fine, put into a ramie-cloth bag, and squeezed dry. Mixed thoroughly with fine salt and stone-ground sesame oil, they make the filling. Naturally, the wrappers use the best wet-milled rice flour.” “Then—” The Seventh Lady was just hungry enough to swallow involuntarily, making Madam Luosi laugh.

“To tell you the truth, Seventh Sister, apart from the Old Lady, everyone finds those plain vegetable dumplings pleasant enough once in a while, but too many of them spoil the appetite,” Madam Luosi said. “Even to me, ours taste quite different from the Xu family’s at Sanpailou.” The wine-lees casserole was an authentic Shanghai dish, one of the so-called local specialities, usually available only at this time. Pork tripe, liver, and other offal were simmered with a plump chicken; when everything was properly cooked, it was poured into an earthenware pot and wine lees were added—hence its name. Wine-lees-cured black carp, cut into pieces and cooked with pale-hearted Chinese cabbage as a soupy dish, was called chuanzao. “And do you think yours is better or worse than Tao A’da’s?” “Worse, of course. Otherwise I should not keep thinking of his.” “I suspect his would not seem quite as good now as you remember.” “Oh? Has the shop changed hands?” “I have never tried those dumplings, but before I fell ill I used to eat Tao A’da’s casserole, outside the county yamen. Last year, when Brother Five came from Songjiang in the twelfth month, he made a special trip there. Their business is flourishing. Why should it change hands?” Madam Luosi was quick to detect a further meaning. “Then there must be a reason for what you said.” The Seventh Lady considered. “I speak my mind, and we are as close as sisters. If I say something mistaken, do not take offence.” “How could I? Seventh Sister, there is no need to say that.”

“There may indeed be some special knack to making those dumplings. But as for the casserole, surely your highly paid master cook is no less skilful than Tao A’da? And as for ingredients, leaving everything else aside, the wine lees you have brought from Shaoxing are better than anything he uses. Your casserole cannot possibly be inferior to his. Yet you say it is. Why?” “Seventh Sister!” Madam Luosi laughed. “That was what I asked you. Why turn the question back on me?” “The casserole, I think, is still the casserole of those days. But Sister Luo is no longer the Sister Luo of those days.” She hastened to add, “I do not mean that you have forgotten your beginnings. I mean that times and circumstances change. Nor did I discover this truth for myself. It came from a story I heard a storyteller tell. In the Tang dynasty there was a monk called Lancan—” She told the story of the monk roasting taro. Madam Luosi certainly felt no suggestion of mockery in it, but she remained silent for a long time, deep in thought.

Ruixiang now brought a maid to lay the table. They helped the Seventh Lady up and lifted her into a specially made round-backed chair. It was large, with brocade cushions packed around her to keep her upright without effort. Across the front lay a broad, removable wooden tray for plates and bowls, edged with a little “wall” five fen high to prevent spilled soup from running everywhere. The chair served much the same purpose as a child’s chair.
